     The difﬁculty has always been to distinguish liquidity crises from sol-
vency crises. The latter require debt restructuring, in which the IMF can
only offer its good ofﬁces, largely by indicating what it considers to be a
manageable debt service burden. But in liquidity crises, such as the one
that hit South Korea in 1997, it makes good sense to lend. The requirement
is simply that the lender of last resort be convinced it will get its money
back.
     An independent IMF staff, headed by a competent and well-respected
management, should be allowed to make such judgments in the event of
liquidity crises. If it turns out that the management has judged wrongly,
they should be ﬁred. But the resources of the IMF are far too limited for it
to be able to play this role effectively. For that reason self-insurance, com-



                                                                  • 189 •
    • Fixing Global Finance



bined with regional reserve pooling arrangements (of which the Chiang
Mai Initiative in Asia is the most important example), seems to be the only
plausible solution.10
     The collapse in IMF lending provides an excellent opportunity to re-
form the institution. It also makes such reform more urgent, since the IMF
is ﬁnanced by the interest paid on its emergency lending. Thus the absence
of international ﬁnancial crises is itself a crisis—for the IMF. Without crises,
there is no lending; without lending, there is no income; and without in-
come, there are no jobs.
     How far has the IMF gone toward necessary reforms? Not far enough.
Peter Kenen, one of the world’s foremost experts on international ﬁnance,
now working at the Council on Foreign Relations in New York, argues that
IMF reform has ﬁve elements, closely related to the challenges outlined
above: the lack of a revenue model in the absence of crisis lending; the
need to make bilateral surveillance more streamlined and effective; the
need to make multilateral surveillance more effective; establishing a new
facility to provide precautionary ﬁnancing for countries that have fol-
lowed prudent policy; and reforming the distribution of IMF quotas. To
this I would add the scale of IMF resources and the need to make selection
of the managing director the outcome of a legitimate global process.11
     On the revenue model, a proposal has been made by a committee ap-
pointed by the managing director that the fund sell up to four hundred
tons of gold and use the proceeds to establish an endowment to ﬁnance
its activities. The idea is attractive, albeit at an early stage.
     On bilateral surveillance, sensible proposals have been made for bi-
ennial analysis of small and stable countries. Surveillance will focus partic-
ularly on ﬁnancial and balance sheet vulnerabilities, as recommended in
Chapter 6.
     Some progress has been made on deﬁning multilateral surveillance
more broadly, with new guidelines suggesting that a country may be con-
sidered to have violated its obligations under the fund’s articles if it is
deemed to maintain an undervalued exchange rate. Needless to say, the
Chinese executive director opposed this idea. But it is at least a small step
in the right direction. So have been the consultations on global imbalances
led by the IMF, with the participation of China, the Eurozone, Japan, Saudi
Arabia, and the United States. But these discussions have not advanced
very far: the big players—China and the United States—merely repeated
pledges they had already made on exchange rate and ﬁscal policies.
     As has been the case in the past, the attempt to create a precautionary
facility for potential borrowers has proved problematic (which is why I
prefer the simpler approach suggested above). But, as Kenen notes, most
countries do not consistently meet plausible criteria. The danger is that,



    • 190 •
                                                    Toward Global Reform •



by publishing data on the deteriorating performance of countries that
had previously qualiﬁed, the facility would create crises rather than reduce
their likelihood. This idea therefore seems to have little chance of making
progress.
     Finally there has been much discussion of changing quotas in the
fund. The new formula will place more weight on GNP, including it at pur-
chasing power parity. But a projected increase of 10 percent in fund quo-
tas cannot bring about much reweighting of the present votes, even if the
twelve countries with the largest quotas all agree to forgo increases.
     There is some agreement that Dominique Strauss-Kahn should be the
last managing director to be chosen in the old Eurocentric way. There is
no agreement at all on the need to make the fund large enough to be rel-
evant in a global crisis. One can only hope that the development of the
domestic-currency-based ﬁnancial system recommended strongly in this
book goes so far that international lending largely ceases to be necessary.
But that seems unlikely. Even if a country does not suffer a large currency
mismatch, it is vulnerable to crisis if it has a large current account deﬁcit
and does not possess one of the world’s dominant currencies, in which it
is always relatively easy to borrow. “Sudden stops” would still be costly un-
der such circumstances. For that reason a larger reserve pool seems highly
attractive. But it also seems highly unlikely.
     What is going to happen to the IMF? It will no doubt continue to make
small improvements. But it is going to remain a marginal institution.
Emerging countries are largely on their own. The IMF can advise, warn,
and cajole, but it cannot deliver much help. Emerging countries should set
their policies accordingly.



    Reform of Informal Groups
     It is in certain informal bodies that the course of much of the world’s
economic discussion is charted. Ideally that would occur within a reformed
IMF. But that is not going to happen. So what should we do about all of the
“Gs”? The G-7 (Canada, France, Germany, Italy, Japan, the United Kingdom,
and the United States) is reasonably like-minded but grossly unrepresenta-
tive of the world as it exists today. The G-20 (Argentina, Australia, Brazil,
Canada, China, the European Union, France, Germany, India, Indonesia,
Italy, Japan, Mexico, Russia, Saudi Arabia, South Africa, South Korea, Turkey,
the United Kingdom, and the United States) is more complete, but some of
its members are not systemically important. It is also surely too large.
     What about creating a G-15? Here are the possible candidates for a
group of countries that have some signiﬁcant systemic role in managing



                                                                  • 191 •
    • Fixing Global Finance



the current system, a group that would certainly make more sense than
the G-7: Brazil, Canada, China, France, Germany, India, Indonesia, Italy,
Japan, Russia, Saudi Arabia, South Korea, the United Kingdom, the United
States, and the European Union. If one were being ruthless, one would ar-
gue that the members of the Eurozone should all be represented by one set
of delegates. Of course, the United Kingdom is not a member of the cur-
rency union. So it should have independent representation.

     What can be expected of the international system? Not much. Creat-
ing a global ﬁnancial and monetary system that is reasonably stable, al-
lows emerging countries to be net recipients of capital without fearing a
devastating crisis at any moment, and does not depend on huge net bor-
rowing by the issuer of the world’s key currency would seem a high enough
priority. But the efforts to do so have been feeble at best. It would help if
the IMF were far better funded for this purpose. It would help too if IMF
multilateral surveillance had teeth—perhaps even the capacity to allow
trade sanctions against those with inordinately mercantilist currency poli-
cies, such as China. But none of this is in the least likely to happen. Emerg-
ing countries must look after themselves. The accumulation of massive re-
serves is an expensive ﬁrst step. It cannot be the last. Creating a sound
domestic-currency-based ﬁnancial system is even more important. The
fate of the emerging countries lies ultimately in their own hands.




    • 192 •
    CHAPTER 8



From Imbalances to the Subprime
Financial Crisis

    If something cannot go on forever, it will stop.
                                             HERBERT STEIN, FORMER CHAIRMAN
                                      OF THE COUNCIL OF ECONOMIC ADVISERS




     ALERT READERS will have noticed that much has changed since this
book was conceived and completed. Indeed, they do not need to be par-
ticularly alert to do so: the biggest ﬁnancial crisis since the 1930s has been
battering the world economy. Moreover, the crisis has not emanated from
emerging countries. Nor has it come from a small number of advanced
countries. This time, the source of the crisis is the United States itself. This
is, therefore, a crisis at the core of the global economy. It generated an ex-
traordinary ﬁnancial panic, a global ﬂight from risk, a steep decline in
global output, a sharp collapse in world trade, a partial disintegration of
the world ﬁnancial system, and a rolling wave of crises in vulnerable
economies across large swaths of the globe.


    The Role of Imbalances in the Crisis
    As I explain in the preface, the argument of this book took shape in
2005 and early 2006. I delivered the series of lectures from which the book
derived at the Paul H. Nitze School of Advanced International Studies of
the Johns Hopkins University in March 2006. The lectures were turned
into a book over the summers of 2006 and 2007 and the early winter of
2007. It was published in October 2008.



                                                                    • 193 •
    • Fixing Global Finance



     What if any relevance, then, do the book’s arguments have to the
world as we know it today?
     I must start with a confession: I did not foresee the scale of this crisis.
Along with many others, I assumed that a ﬁnancial crisis comparable to
that of the early 1930s would not be repeated in my lifetime. We now know
better. Indeed, to his credit, the late Andrew Glyn of Corpus Christi Col-
lege, Oxford, whose ﬁnal book I cite in the preface, already knew better.1
The most sophisticated modern ﬁnancial systems are, it turns out, as vul-
nerable to the frailties discussed in Chapter 2 as those of emerging coun-
tries today or of the United States in the 1930s. Indeed, the grim fact that
ﬁnancial crises (though not public debt crises) afﬂict rich countries as
much as poor ones is one of the most striking conclusions of the exhaus-
tive analysis of centuries of ﬁnancial crises provided in a seminal new book
by Carmen Reinhart of the University of Maryland and Kenneth Rogoff of
Harvard University.2 Financial deregulation, they also note, continues to
spawn crises—as it always has.
     This book focuses on the stresses imposed by macroeconomic forces,
particularly the global savings glut and associated imbalances in global
payments. The analysis looks at the implications for monetary policy and
examines the consequences of these underlying forces for domestic debt,
particularly of U.S. households (see Chapter 4). This analysis was correct,
as far as it went. But it did not go far enough. In particular, it did not fo-
cus sufﬁciently on what was going on inside the ﬁnancial sector, mainly
the massive rise in leverage and the way the “originate and distribute,”
transaction-oriented, model of securitized ﬁnance worked—or rather did
not work. Financial crises lie at the intersection between macroeconomic
and microeconomic forces. In the previous edition of this book, the analy-
sis of the former was more complete than that of the latter. That turned
out to be an error.
     Yet my view that global imbalances had anything to do with this crisis
is widely rejected. This also is a huge mistake. Chinese policy makers resent
the implication that their policies bear any part of the blame for what they
condemn as the arrogant incompetence of the United States.3 Michael Doo-
ley and Peter Garber—two of the authors of the inﬂuential Bretton Woods
Two hypothesis, with which I have already indicated substantial disagree-
ment (pp. 141–46)—also reject the idea out of hand. They insist that the
crisis that hit the ﬁnancial system had nothing to do with the savings sur-
pluses of emerging countries, notably China, and the consequent reduction
in global real interest rates to very low levels. In particular, they argue, “the
idea that an excessive compression of spreads and increased leverage were
directly caused by the low real interest rates seems to us entirely without
foundation.” Dooley and Garber, along with Governor Zhou Xiaochuan of



    • 194 •
                              From Imbalances to the Subprime Financial Crisis •



the People’s Bank of China, contend that the blame lies “with ineffective
supervision and regulation of ﬁnancial markets in the U.S. and other in-
dustrial countries driven by ill-conceived policy choices.”4
     Meanwhile, other well-known economists—notably John Taylor, ac-
cording to whose Taylor Rule monetary policy is explained by deviations
of the actual inﬂation rate from the target inﬂation rate and of actual GDP
from potential GDP—also reject the idea that global imbalances or the sav-
ings glut had anything to do with this crisis. They instead blame the overly
lax monetary policies of the Federal Reserve.5
     So what is the truth? The already-extensive literature on the crisis puts
forward six broad explanations: global imbalances or the savings glut
(themselves driven by mercantilist trade policies, by the desire to reduce
the risk of a ﬁnancial crisis, or by a search for high-quality ﬁnancial assets);
lax monetary policy in the United States and other advanced countries
(the latter partly, or even largely, in response to U.S. policies); the greed
and immorality of those engaged in ﬁnancial activities; the inherent in-
stability of ﬁnancial capitalism, with its swings from euphoria to panic; ir-
responsible use of ﬁnancial innovations;6 and government malfeasance,
namely, acts of regulatory omission (failures to regulate) and commission
(subsidization—or active promotion—of bad lending).7 Not surprisingly,
authors choose explanations that ﬁt their own particular intellectual and
personal prejudices and preferences.
     All this reminds me of the well-known story in which four people are
invited to go into a dark room, touch what is there, and report back on
what they have found. One says it is a leather sail. Another says it is a pull
rope. Another says it is a tree. Yet another says it is a huge snake. It is, of
course, an elephant. The crisis, too, is an elephant and, like the elephant,
it is not so simple to describe. Some explanations seem useless—for ex-
ample, greed, which is always present in human affairs and cannot explain
any particular event, or the instability of ﬁnancial capitalism, which is also
a background condition, not an explanation for particular events at par-
ticular times. Yet most of these explanations do have some part to play in
answering the big questions: Why now? Why here? Why so huge?
     The rest of the chapter offers my answers to these three big questions.
It starts with a discussion of why global macroeconomic forces matter. This
leads to an explanation of how and why the crisis happened. An analysis
of the challenges the crisis poses for global adjustment follows. In partic-
ular, purposeful adjustment is needed in both deﬁcit and surplus countries
if the outcome is not to be the destruction of the credit of the governments
of some of the world’s most economically important countries, and par-
ticularly of the United States. But if only the deﬁcit countries adjust, the
world economy faces the danger of years of slow growth—or even worse,



                                                                       • 195 •
    • Fixing Global Finance



a repetition at the global level of what has happened in Japan in the past
two decades. Finally, the discussion turns to how far the recommendations
made in Chapters 6 and 7 need to be modiﬁed in light of the crisis. The
conclusion, in essence, is that they do not: but they are even more urgent
than before.



    Why Imbalances Mattered
     My explanation of the crisis would indeed give a big role to global im-
balances and the exceptional macroeconomic and ﬁnancial conditions
they created in the 2000s. Those forces played a role in four different ways.
     The ﬁrst is via the impact of an aggregate savings glut on asset prices.
Taylor rejects the notion of such a glut, because global savings as a share
of output have been falling.8 It is also true, by deﬁnition, that savings
equals investment at the world level. Yet there is one quite straightforward
and widely accepted empirical deﬁnition of such a glut—low long-term
real interest rates.9 As I have noted (see Figure 4.2), we have been living in
just such a world. This has been particularly surprising because the 2000s
were, prior to the crisis, a period of rapid global growth—an era, therefore,
when real interest rates would be expected to be high.
     Low real interest rates should lead to high prices for real assets, as in-
deed happened in the case of real estate in the United States and a number
of other high-income countries. Higher equilibrium prices for real assets
do not themselves cause ﬁnancial crises. But they are, as we shall see shortly,
a plausible trigger—what the late Hyman Minsky called a “displacement”
—for a process leading to such a crisis.10 Indeed, Minsky’s account of ﬁ-
nancial crises sheds great light on what happened. Furthermore, as Doo-
ley and Garber agree, low long-term real interest rates cannot be credibly
explained by a lax monetary policy, which, by its nature, only affects short-
term nominal interest rates. They are best explained by underlying real
forces. The savings glut—which, as I have noted, might be better called an
investment dearth—is just such an explanation.
     The second way of looking at this question, central to the argument
advanced in this book, is Keynesian (a label somewhat less objectionable
to “right-thinking” economists today than it has been for decades). This
view focuses on patterns of income and expenditure across the globe. The
relevant Keynesian question is how excess desired savings in some parts of
the world are not just offset, but offset at levels of spending adequate to
sustain vigorous expansion of the world economy. The answer is again
clear. As argued in Chapter 4, the United States above all, but also a num-
ber of other high-income countries, imported and then offset, through



    • 196 •
                             From Imbalances to the Subprime Financial Crisis •



their excess of spending over income, the excess savings of the rest of the
world.11 The one part of the emerging world that also ran substantial
current account deﬁcits was central and eastern Europe—with dire conse-
quences during the 2007–9 crisis, as one might have expected from the
long and painful history of sudden stops in lending to emerging countries
that was discussed in Chapter 3. This way of looking at the world econ-
omy is as heterodox as that of Minsky. But it has the beneﬁt of forcing us
to focus on the sustainability of spending, particularly in terms of the as-
sociated rise in liabilities.12
     Furthermore, if a country is running a current account deﬁcit, some
actors within the economy must also be spending more than their incomes
and so must be selling net ﬁnancial claims to the rest of the world. This is
least worrying if the spending takes the form of corporate investment, par-
ticularly in the production of tradable goods and services. More often,
however, the domestic counterpart takes the form of excess consumption,
public or private. If it is the former, the counterpart of the external bor-
rowing will take the form of a large ﬁscal deﬁcit. If it is the latter, it will
take the form of credit-fueled household spending. Often there is also a
boom in commercial property, residential property, or both. Such a time
of easy credit also means increases in leverage throughout the economy
and consequent increases in ﬁnancial fragility.
     In the case of the United States, the most important of the deﬁcit coun-
tries, the unprecedented current account deﬁcits of the 2000s had, as their
domestic counterpart, not just a sizable ﬁscal deﬁcit, triggered by the tax
cuts and spending promoted by George W. Bush, but also unprecedented
excesses of spending over incomes in the U.S. household sector (see Figure
4.30). This excessive spending was also associated with an exceptional rise
in gross household indebtedness (see Figure 4.31). It is easy to see why
these developments exacerbated ﬁnancial fragility. Indeed, the book by
Reinhart and Rogoff, which studies centuries of ﬁnancial history, demon-
strates that they have all too often been the precursors of ﬁnancial crises.13
This may not be what theory predicts. But experience trumps theory.
     Many policy makers and even some economists, to my astonishment,
continue to talk of the surplus savings in one set of countries and the ex-
cess spending in others as if they had nothing to do with one another. This
is ridiculous. The former logically entail the latter. If excess saving is vir-
tuous, how can the excess spending that makes it possible be a vice? Is every-
body supposed to run vast current account surpluses? If so, with whom
—Martians? And if everybody does indeed try to run a savings surplus,
what else can the outcome be but a permanent global depression?
     The third way of looking at the role of imbalances is in terms of driv-
ers of behavior. Identities, such as those just discussed, tell one about



                                                                      • 197 •
    • Fixing Global Finance



macroeconomic consistency, but they say nothing about causality. There
are many possibilities. But I have suggested (pp. 100–101) what I see as the
mechanism:
    One can explain what is going on, simply put, as follows: The rest of the
    world’s capital outﬂow supports the dollar. At the resulting elevated real
    exchange rate for the United States, the output of the sectors in the U.S.
    economy that produce tradable goods and services shrinks, other things
    being equal. The Federal Reserve cuts interest rates to expand the econ-
    omy, thereby preventing excessive unemployment. As it does so, a large
    excess demand for tradable goods and services emerges in the United
    States. This, ﬁnally, appears in the trade and current account deﬁcits.
         One consequence of all this is that U.S. domestic demand has had
    to grow faster than real GDP, to ensure that the latter grows in line with
    potential. The difference between the two is, of course, the increase in
    the current account deﬁcit, in real terms. With trend growth in GDP be-
    tween 3 and 3.5 percent a year, domestic demand has had to grow even
    faster. That is precisely what has happened. U.S. real demand (or “gross
    domestic purchases”) grew faster than real GDP in 1993 and 1994 and
    then again in every year from 1996 to 2004 inclusive. . . . Cumulatively,
    between 1993 and 2004 U.S. real GDP grew by 46 percent, while gross
    domestic purchases rose by 53 percent. That is how the current account
    deﬁcit emerged. It is also how the U.S. absorbed the supply of excess
    capital from abroad.

     As Michael Pettis of Peking University summarizes my own argument,
the Federal Reserve had to choose between unemployment and indebted-
ness (both external and domestic).14 It chose the latter. Indeed, it was cer-
tain to choose the latter, because its mandate is to sustain the highest level
of employment consistent with price stability (or, more precisely, stable
and low inﬂation). Since the United States has no exchange rate policy and
has been able to borrow freely in its own currency, and since, in addition,
countries that target exchange rates usually do so against the dollar, the
Federal Reserve emerges automatically as the world’s macroeconomic bal-
ancer (and the United States as the economy inside which global balanc-
ing takes place). Meanwhile, the countries intervening in foreign exchange
markets and exporting savings had to run their monetary, credit, and ﬁs-
cal policies in ways that stabilized their own economies. This meant en-
suring that the domestic consequences of export surpluses, including the
monetary results of currency interventions, were offset or sterilized, to
avoid generating chronic excess demand.
     The ﬁnal way of looking at the imbalances is via the capital account.
We need to understand how the accumulation of safe assets, largely by for-
eign governments, was transformed into the creation of a house-price bub-
ble and a huge increase of leverage within the United States, which then
triggered the ﬁnancial crisis. The most thought-provoking analysis of these



    • 198 •
                              From Imbalances to the Subprime Financial Crisis •



developments I have seen is by Anton Brender and Florence Pisani of Paris
Dauphine.15 In essence, they argue, foreign governments accumulated as-
sets on which they took the foreign currency risk, a fact that the Chinese
government now ﬁnds objectionable. But, so far as they could, they took
no other risks. They were looking for riskless assets.
     Such assets could, in principle, have been supplied by an AAA-rated
government, such as the federal government of the United States. But, in
practice, it did not borrow on the needed scale. So ways had to be found
of converting these safe external claims into riskier domestic claims (in-
cluding what turned out to be “pseudo-AAA-rated” private claims). More-
over, these ways also had to generate the additional demand needed to off-
set the current account deﬁcits. That is what the housing bubble and
associated securitized mortgage ﬁnance did. Thus these bubbles were not
an extraneous and incomprehensible occurrence. They were an essential
part of the process of creating and ﬁnancing the global imbalances.
     Brender and Pisani argue:
    The emerging region’s savings surplus could not have been built up if
    there had not been a counterpart in the form of an increased ﬁnancial
    requirement in the developed countries. However, it was not sufﬁcient
    for the latter to import those savings. Since the emerging region’s sur-
    plus was for the most part invested risk-free, it was necessary that the
    developed regions take on the risks that the emerging regions did not.
    In order that the savings invested risk free should ﬁnance investments
    that are risky by nature, someone somewhere had to take on the associ-
    ated risks. That has probably been the most original, and the least em-
    phasized, contribution of globalization. By considerably facilitating the
    circulation of ﬁnancial risks, it enabled the developed world to relieve
    the emerging countries of a signiﬁcant part of those related to the in-
    vesting of their savings surplus—at the cost, obviously, of the accumula-
    tion of risks in the globalized ﬁnancial system.16

     The argument, then, is that the excess spending of U.S. households,
which was a crucial counterpart of the country’s current account deﬁcit,
was ﬁnanced by increasingly risky mortgages. Meanwhile, the external ﬁ-
nance was largely in the form of low-risk lending, apart from the currency
risk inherent in lending in foreign currencies. The ﬁnancial system in the
United States, then, had to convert the foreign loans into ﬁnancing of the
riskier domestic assets. In doing so it inevitably accumulated a great deal
of risk. But it did so in ways perfectly designed to conceal that risk—from
the point of view of almost every player in the crisis.
     What those of us who focused on the macroeconomic side of the story
missed was that the big risks being accumulated would turn out not to be
the risk to the dollar, but credit and liquidity risks within the United States.
Foreign governments would continue to purchase what they saw as safe



                                                                       • 199 •
    • Fixing Global Finance



U.S. government dollar-denominated liabilities, including those issued by
government-sponsored enterprises—which, they rightly believed, would
be bailed out. The trick was ultimately exposed, and much of the risk fell
on taxpayers. While emerging economies that suffer ﬁnancial crises after
a big foreign-ﬁnanced spending boom usually suffer both a currency crisis
and a domestic ﬁnancial crisis, the United States, as the issuer of the
world’s most important currency, does not. The difference is extremely im-
portant. It largely determines the immediate outcome of the crisis. But it
may not hold forever—a point to which I will return.



    The Run-Up to the Crisis
     With these elements in mind, let us consider my story of the crisis. It
would start with four background elements that created the conditions for
an exceptional mania: regulatory failure, ﬁnancial innovation, the China
shock, and the great moderation.
     By regulatory failure I refer to acts of both commission and omission.
The acts of commission were the strong incentives for homeownership,
particularly in the United States; these included the promotion of lending
to low-income borrowers, the weakness of bankruptcy procedures, and the
vast moral hazard inherent in the ambiguous status of the so-called gov-
ernment-sponsored enterprises, particularly Fannie Mae and Freddie Mac.
The acts of omission were the willingness to accept an explosion of off-
balance-sheet vehicles, derivatives, and structured lending, largely as ways
around capital requirements. A central part of this process was undue re-
liance on the rating agencies, themselves subject to perverse incentives.
     By ﬁnancial innovation I refer, in particular, to the unbundling of—
and trading in—the different components of lending activities. Particularly
important innovations in this crisis were the extension of securitization to
a wide range of ﬁnance; structured ﬁnance, with its promise of turning sub-
prime loans into AAA-rated claims; and the explosion of the essentially un-
regulated credit default swap market, which made people believe they had
adequately insured their risks when they had not. These innovations al-
lowed the explosive growth of the shadow banking system, in which clas-
sic term transformation functions of commercial banks were exercised, on
a gigantic scale, by unregulated ﬁnancial institutions.
     By the China shock I refer to three effects of the explosive growth of
the world’s most populous country: the falling dollar prices of a large range
of labor-intensive manufactures, which kept global inﬂation low and
helped make the job of inﬂation-targeting central banks easier; the impact
of growing Chinese demand on commodity prices, including the price of



    • 200 •
                             From Imbalances to the Subprime Financial Crisis •



oil and so the current account surpluses of oil-exporting countries; and the
rapid rise of the Chinese current account surplus itself.
     Finally, by the great moderation I refer to growing conﬁdence in a
world of low and stable inﬂation and low macroeconomic volatility. Ben
Bernanke gave an inﬂuential and, as it turned out, perilous speech on this
idea in 2004.17 In it he claimed that improved monetary policy had played
a signiﬁcant part in this development. This is a rather less plausible hy-
pothesis today.
     The economic story of the 2000s thus starts with the savings glut, as I
argue in the book. What lay behind the savings glut? The ﬁrst develop-
ment was the shift of emerging economies into a large surplus of savings
over investment. Within the emerging economies, the big shifts were in
Asia and in the oil-exporting countries. By 2007, according to the IMF, the
aggregate savings surpluses of these two groups of countries had reached
around 2 percent of world output. Despite being a huge oil importer, China
emerged as the world’s biggest surplus country: its current account surplus
was $372 billion, which was not only more than 11 percent of its GDP but
also almost as big as the combined surpluses of Germany ($253 billion)
and Japan ($211 billion), the two largest high-income capital exporters.
     In 2007, according to the IMF, the aggregate surpluses of the world’s
surplus countries reached the staggering total of $1.743 trillion. The top ten
(China, Germany, Japan, Saudi Arabia, Russia, Switzerland, Norway, the
Netherlands, Kuwait, and Singapore) generated more than 70 percent of
this total. The surpluses of the ten largest surplus countries represented at
least 8 percent of their aggregate GDPs and about a quarter of their aggre-
gate gross savings.
     Moreover, at least in many emerging countries, the rise of the current
account surpluses—which are also, by deﬁnition, surpluses of savings over
investment or income over spending—was in part the result of deliberate
policies—as shown in the accumulation of ofﬁcial foreign currency reserves
and the expansion of the sovereign wealth funds over this period. Quite
reasonably, the energy exporters were transforming one asset—oil—into
another—claims on foreigners. Others were recycling current account sur-
pluses and private capital inﬂows into ofﬁcial capital outﬂows, keeping ex-
change rates down and competitiveness up.
     Some described this new system, of which China was the most im-
portant proponent, as Bretton Woods Two, after the pegged adjustable
exchange rate set-up that collapsed in the early 1970s. Others called it ex-
port-led growth or depicted it as a system of self-insurance. Yet the justiﬁ-
cation is less important than the consequences. Between January 2000 and
April 2007, the stock of global foreign currency reserves rose by $5.2 tril-
lion, with China alone accumulating an extra $1.6 trillion, bringing its to-



                                                                      • 201 •
    • Fixing Global Finance



tal to $1.76 trillion. Three-quarters of all foreign currency reserves accu-
mulated have been piled up in this decade. Inevitably, a high proportion—
probably close to two-thirds—of these sums was placed in dollars, thereby
supporting the U.S. currency and ﬁnancing U.S. external deﬁcits.
     Yet the savings glut had another dimension, related to a second big ﬁ-
nancial shock—the bursting of the dot-com bubble in 2000. One conse-
quence was the move of the corporate sectors of most high-income coun-
tries into ﬁnancial surplus. In other words, their retained earnings came to
exceed their investments. Instead of borrowing from banks and other sup-
pliers of capital, nonﬁnancial corporations became providers of ﬁnance.
     In this world of massive savings surpluses, real interest rates were low.
This helped trigger a rise in house prices in many high-income countries,
including the United States. Real home prices increased nearly 50 percent
in the United States between 1998 and 2006, while they increased more
than 130 percent in Ireland, 120 percent in the United Kingdom and
Spain, and over 100 percent in France.18 Whether it was aware of it or not,
the Federal Reserve’s monetary policy was intended to offset the drag on
activity caused by the collapse in stock prices after 2000, the consequent
weakness in domestic corporate investment, and the rise in the external
deﬁcit. While short-term interest rates might have been too low, there was
good reason for them to be lower than one might normally have ex-
pected.19 The combination of low long-term and short-term real and nom-
inal interest rates with rising house prices touched off a borrowing and
spending boom among households, as well as a boom in residential in-
vestment. Because inﬂation and inﬂationary expectations were well con-
tained, the Federal Reserve and other central banks were able to sustain ac-
commodative policies for a lengthy period.
     This then was Minsky’s displacement. It started the process of em-
bracing risk and increasing leverage that ultimately led to the crisis. It was
marked by increasingly irresponsible lending and borrowing, all covered
by rising asset prices. Indeed, many observers in the United States assumed
that house prices could not—or at least would not—fall. The vested inter-
ests of those who believed in such propositions, or at least appeared to be-
lieve in them—highly leveraged home buyers, realtors, builders, loan orig-
inators, ﬁnanciers, raters, and even politicians—were too strong to
override. This, too, is a classic symptom of a mania. No doubt many par-
ticipants were not fooled. But those who were not fooled did not have the
incentive to blow the whistle. For many, the gamble was worth taking. For
many, indeed, it was a case of “heads I win and tails you lose”—the ulti-
mate losers being outsiders.
     As the world economy grew at an exceptionally rapid rate, there
emerged a symbiotic interest in the ﬁnance of the bubble economy in the



    • 202 •
                             From Imbalances to the Subprime Financial Crisis •



United States and elsewhere. Emerging countries that wanted to pursue
export-oriented development policies and accumulate safe reserve assets,
as insurance, were able to run large current account surpluses and grow
rapidly. Commodity exporters were able to ﬁnd what appeared to be a
good home for their savings. Meanwhile, there was an offsetting emer-
gence of the United States and a number of other high-income countries
as spenders and borrowers of last resort.
     Reinhart and Rogoff stress the similarity between the present crisis and
earlier crises in high-income countries, notably those in Scandinavia and
Japan in the 1990s.20 But they also note similarities with earlier emerging
market crises, not least the ﬁrst big one of modern times—the Latin Amer-
ican debt crisis of the early 1980s, already touched upon in Chapter 3. In
the run-up to that crisis, the so-called petrodollars (the foreign exchange
earned by oil-exporting countries amid sharp jumps in the crude price)
were recycled, via Western banks, to emerging economies, principally in
Latin America. This resulted in the ﬁrst of the big crises of modern times,
when Mexico’s announcement in 1982 of its inability to service its debt
brought the money-center banks of New York and London to their knees.
     This time, however, note Reinhart and Rogoff, a “large chunk of money
has effectively been recycled to a developing economy that exists within
the United States’ own borders. Over a trillion dollars was channeled into
the subprime mortgage market, which is comprised of the poorest and
least creditworthy borrowers within the United States. The ﬁnal claimant
is different, but in many ways the mechanism is the same.”21 In this case,
surplus savings were not recycled into emerging countries, but into ad-
vanced countries with highly elastic credit systems that supported—and
were supported by—unsustainable rises in asset prices.
     What was happening on the capital-exporting side of the imbalances
is easy enough to see, even if the accumulation of reserves and reliance on
export growth looks unwise (see Chapter 6). But three important questions
do arise about the capital importers and particularly about the United
States.
     The ﬁrst such question concerns the link between declining real in-
terest rates and the “reach for yield” or greater willingness to take risks. I
would stress both the demand and the supply side.
     On the demand side, many investors needed higher real returns than
safe government bonds provided, while stocks looked less attractive after
the collapse of the market in 2000. There was, therefore, a large market
for higher-yielding AAA-rated assets. This is the role innovation played: it
provided buyers, across the world, with what they were looking for, on
a gigantic scale, via structured ﬁnance. As Lloyd Blankfein, chairman of
Goldman Sachs, points out, “in January 2008, there were 12 triple A–rated



                                                                      • 203 •
    • Fixing Global Finance



companies in the world. At the same time, there were 64,000 structured ﬁ-
nance instruments . . . rated triple A.”22 These ﬁgures are breathtaking.
     In short, what the market demanded, the market supplied. Overall de-
mand for high-grade assets exceeded the supply, partly because of the
emergence of the current account surpluses.23 This drove down yields. The
market’s imaginative, albeit temporary, response was to mass-produce er-
satz high-grade assets. In a brilliant inversion of Joseph Schumpeter’s idea
of creative destruction, Jagdish Bhagwati of Columbia University calls this
destructive creation.24
     Associated with these new assets was a new ﬁnancial system—one that
evolved outside the banking system, partly to evade regulatory constraints
on capital. But, enjoying no government guarantees and no lender of last
resort, this shadow banking system was an accident waiting to happen.
The theory was that risk had been unbundled and could now be allocated
to those best able to bear it. In practice, however, risk was largely borne by
those least able to understand it. Extraordinarily, among these foolish risk
bearers were many of the creators of the new instruments. They drowned
in their own toxic assets.25
     All this suggests more than a small amount of insouciance on the part
of investors. But here one has to remember the belief in the great moder-
ation, in the wisdom and effectiveness of monetary policymaking, in the
virtues of ﬁnancial innovation, in the advantages of the new transaction-
oriented ﬁnancial system, in the impossibility of identifying bubbles be-
fore they burst, and in the self-stabilizing capacities of the ﬁnancial mar-
kets. On the latter, Alan Greenspan famously told Congress in October
2008, “those of us who have looked to the self-interest of lending institu-
tions to protect shareholder’s equity (myself especially) are in a state of
shocked disbelief. Such counterparty surveillance is a central pillar of our
ﬁnancial markets’ state of balance. If it fails, as occurred this year, market
stability is undermined.”26
     The second big question concerns the role of rising leverage in the sys-
tem. The answer is that it was everywhere. The new structured ﬁnance was
inherently highly leveraged, with the division of the cash ﬂows generated
by servicing of mortgage debt into different tranches. Leverage was added
by the operation of the shadow banking system—conduits, special invest-
ment vehicles, and money market funds—all of which operated with next
to no capital and indeed largely in order to avoid using valuable and ex-
pensive capital. Low nominal and real short- and long-term interest rates
in an environment of great conﬁdence in low inﬂation and economic sta-
bility encouraged further leverage inside and outside the ﬁnancial system.
Low interest rates also raised the prices of nominal assets, such as high-
grade bonds, which improved the capitalization of ﬁnancial institutions



    • 204 •
                                      From Imbalances to the Subprime Financial Crisis •



and their balance sheets, thereby supporting more leverage. Meanwhile,
among nonﬁnancial borrowers, particularly households, rising prices of
real estate improved the collateral they could offer against loans. As real
estate prices continued to rise, lending and borrowing became increasingly
speculative, the expectation being that rising prices would allow borrow-
ers to pay off their loans and reap substantial returns to their often negli-
gible equity, as Minsky would have expected.
     The rising leverage is evident at the economy-wide level (Figure 8.1).
In 1981 the ratio of total gross debt to U.S. GDP was just 161 percent. By
2000 it had reached 268 percent. But by the ﬁrst quarter of 2009 it had
reached 362 percent. Nearly all this increase was in the private sector, par-
ticularly the household and ﬁnancial sectors. Thus the overall ratio of pri-

    • Fixing Global Finance



vate gross debt to GDP rose from 123 percent in 1981 to 222 percent in
2000 and 298 percent by the ﬁrst quarter of 2009. The ratio of household
gross debt to GDP jumped from 48 percent in 1981 to 71 percent in 2000
and 98 percent in the ﬁrst quarter of 2009. Meanwhile, the ratio of gross
ﬁnancial sector indebtedness—a measure of leverage within the sector—
exploded from 22 percent of GDP in 1981 to 83 percent in 2000 and 121
percent in the ﬁrst quarter of 2009.27
     The huge increase in leverage has been continuing for three decades.
But it increased particularly rapidly when the current account deﬁcit ex-
ploded, especially in the case of households. But what has this do with the
savings glut and global imbalances? There are at least four connections:
First, these global forces helped create the interest rate conditions for in-
creasing leverage. Second, they increased the incentive for leverage, as a
means of raising returns on notionally safe assets. Third, as Brender and
Pisani argue, they created a need to transform safe foreign liabilities into
risky domestic assets, the by-product of which was rising leverage inside
the economy. And, last and most important, rising leverage both stimu-
lated and was a consequence of rising house prices, which permitted
households to spend part of their (rising) equity and also supported a
boom in home building. The rise in asset prices more than offset the rise
in indebtedness between 2002 and 2006: the ratio of household net worth
to disposable incomes rose from 518 percent in 2002 to 646 percent in
2006.28 So long as asset prices remained high, everything looked perfectly
safe. But, as everybody knows, they did not: by the end of 2008, house-
hold net worth had collapsed to 483 percent of GDP, with dire results.
     The last of the three questions concerns what would have happened
if the rise in leverage had been prevented, as many believe should have
happened, either by tighter regulation or by higher short-term interest rates
or by both methods applied simultaneously. It is hard to analyze such a
counterfactual. It is possible that bad lending and irresponsible leverage
would have been eliminated, without reducing aggregate spending. It is
more likely that demand would have been signiﬁcantly weaker. But the
growth of the U.S. economy was not particularly dynamic in the 2000s,
and there was no obvious sign of overheating, apart from what was hap-
pening in the ﬁnancial sector: average annual GDP growth between 2002
and 2006 (inclusive) was only 2.7 percent. In 2004, the year that saw the
fastest growth in the 2000s, the rate reached only 3.6 percent. Signs of
overheating were limited to rising commodity prices, which had little to
do with what was happening in the United States. Core inﬂation appeared,
to the Federal Reserve at least, to be well under control.
     In short, if successful efforts had been made to constrain rising lever-
age and irresponsible lending for housing, it is highly likely that the Fed-



    • 206 •
                             From Imbalances to the Subprime Financial Crisis •



eral Reserve would have felt obliged to follow an even easier monetary pol-
icy. If so, additional borrowing would presumably have occurred some-
where else in the economy. So long as the country continued to have a
large structural current account deﬁcit, demand had to be sustained at a
level high enough to offset it and keep GDP in line with potential. If it had
not been sustained in the way it was, one has to ask how else it could have
been.
     The preconditions for this crisis were many. It is no simple story. But I
remain convinced, for the reasons already explained, that the pattern of
global imbalances and associated policies played a large role in setting the
explosive charge, even if they did not set it off. The bottom line is simple:
So long as some countries follow policies that generate huge current account
surpluses, others must run huge deﬁcits. If such deﬁcits are to exist, some
sectors of the economy must spend substantially more than their incomes.
In this case, the sector that turned out to be most vulnerable was house-
holds. Rising asset prices and leverage, supported by ﬁnancial innovation,
made this happen. If they had not done so, some other way would have had
to be found to achieve much the same outcome. At the end of this lengthy
story, the economy had become extremely fragile ﬁnancially. Indeed, such
is the role of the United States in the world economy that the fragility was
spread across much of the world. Finally, house prices started to decline, bad
debt started to rise, risk started to be repriced, and worries about funding
highly leveraged institutions, with portfolios of uncertain value, started to
spread. The stage was set for a panic, and in the end that was what hit, start-
ing in the summer of 2007 and reaching a crescendo in the autumn of 2008.
The worst ﬁnancial crisis since the 1930s was under way.29
     My view, then, is that the United States has been as much the victim
of decisions made by others as the author of its own misfortunes. That is
a desperately unpopular view—in the United States, because it is easier for
most Americans to accept that they are incompetent than that they are im-
potent, and in the rest of the world, because it is far easier to accept that
the United States is guilty than that others share the responsibility. But the
principal argument of this book is that the crisis is a global predicament
explained by decisions—and frailties—across the globe. The conclusion is
also that a part of the answer to the crisis is to limit the scale of macro-
economic imbalances, since they frequently exacerbate ﬁnancial fragility.



    After the Crisis
    We will never know whether the story laid out here had to lead to so
severe a crisis. But a signiﬁcant correction seems to have been inevitable.



                                                                      • 207 •
    • Fixing Global Finance



At some point the period of rising asset prices, underestimation of risk, and
rising leverage would be replaced by its opposite. Previous experience across
the world suggests that such corrections can be brutal and swift. Panics—
what economists specializing in emerging countries call sudden stops—are
frequent events. Just such a stop happened in the global ﬁnancial markets
in September and October 2008. This followed the collapse of Lehman
Brothers and the rescue of American International Group. But markets had
already been under substantial stress in the previous thirteen months. At
some stage, massive government intervention was probably going to be re-
quired. It would take a crisis to cause such an intervention. If so, the
Lehman collapse was merely the trigger for that event. If it had not hap-
pened, something else would probably have had to take its place.
     The most important policy decision was reached at the meeting of the
ﬁnance ministers and central bank governors of the G-7 in October 2007.
The G-7 agreed to “take decisive action and use all available tools to sup-
port systemically important ﬁnancial institutions and prevent their fail-
ure.”30 With that decision, the risks in the ﬁnancial system were socialized.
In addition, decisions were reached to push for a sizable ﬁscal stimulus.
Meanwhile, central banks moved rapidly toward massive monetary easing.
In all, ﬁscal and monetary policies were more expansionary than they had
ever been in peacetime, far more so than in the 1930s—fortunately.
     A ﬁnancial crisis in the United States is different from a crisis in emerg-
ing countries, in several crucial respects. The government of the United
States remains creditworthy. Indeed, during the panic, money ﬂew into
dollars, not away from them. Other developed countries, though not in as
strong a position as the United States, are also able to borrow in their own
currencies on favorable terms. Thus a ﬁnancial crisis in developed coun-
tries is not, as it often is in emerging countries, also a currency and even a
ﬁscal crisis, at least in the short to medium run. These states are able to res-
cue the ﬁnancial system by taking the dramatic monetary and ﬁscal meas-
ures the world has seen.
     A possible conclusion would be that these characteristics of developed
countries make such crises manageable. However big the short-term dis-
ruption might be, governments of high-income countries can deal with it
by using their own balance sheets to replace, or bolster, the battered bal-
ance sheets of the private sector. If so, proponents of Bretton Woods Two
would argue, it will soon be possible to return to business as usual, with
emerging countries exporting to their traditional markets. Instead of the
desired AAA-rated assets being fabricated by the private sector, they can
come from the best possible source—governments themselves.
     By the summer of 2009, the world economy was on the mend. The ﬁ-
nancial panic had faded and demand was recovering. It was already possi-



    • 208 •
                              From Imbalances to the Subprime Financial Crisis •



ble to start talking about exit strategies from the exceptional easing after
the crisis. It was also possible to look anew at the structural ﬁscal weaknesses
that had emerged in previous deﬁcit countries, particularly the United
States and the United Kingdom, when their housing markets and ﬁnancial
sectors crashed. For what had happened was precisely what some of those
concerned about the long-term sustainability of large current account
deﬁcits had feared: the emergence of massive, structural ﬁscal deﬁcits.
    The events demonstrated a fundamental point made in this book—a
big swing in private sector spending is sure to create an offsetting shift in
the ﬁscal position, unless there is an extremely swift adjustment in the ex-
ternal balance. The latter is difﬁcult and usually takes substantial time to
achieve, particularly for a big, closed economy like that of the United States,
and particularly during a deep global recession.
    The result emerges clearly from Figure 8.2, which is based on forecasts
of current accounts and general government ﬁscal balances from the Eu-
ropean Commission. From this it is easy to derive the shift in the balance
between income and spending in the private sector, since, by deﬁnition,
the private sector balance, the ﬁscal balance, and the net supply of foreign
capital (the inverse of the current account deﬁcit) must add to zero. What
emerges is that the United States, the United Kingdom, and Spain, all of
which had large housing bubbles, are forecast to experience a huge swing
in the balance between private income and spending, largely offset by a
massive opposite swing in the ﬁscal balance, with relatively modest changes
in the net inﬂow of foreign capital. Meanwhile, Japan, France, and partic-
ularly Germany are forecast to experience relatively small swings in the
private balance, because the private sectors in those countries were far less
exposed at the beginning. Instead, they are forecast to suffer substantial
losses on the current account, again particularly in the case of Germany.
These shifts are offset by smaller deteriorations in the ﬁscal position.
    For countries with large structural current account deﬁcits and mas-
sive retrenchment in the private sector, huge ﬁscal deﬁcits are inevitable.
In the United States and United Kingdom, the deﬁcits were expected to ex-
ceed 10 percent of GDP in 2009. These deﬁcits are likely to remain very
large for a substantial period of time, and public debt ratios are likely to
explode. They can improve, while the economy returns to something close
to potential output, if and only if the private sector starts to spend freely
again or the current account deﬁcit improves sharply. Moreover, the latter
improvement must be structural, not merely cyclical. Of course, the
United States can improve its ﬁscal deﬁcit by following the ﬁscal policies
of Herbert Hoover, as many recommend. But what would be the good of
that, if it merely pushed the domestic economy and possibly even the
world economy into depression?

     Thus the external adjustment that is the theme of this chapter and the
preceding two is even more urgent now that the crisis has come. Making
that adjustment will determine the nature of the world’s escape from the
crisis. Assume that some years from now the United States is still unable
to run a sizable current account surplus, at full employment. The country
will then have a few rather unpleasant choices: it can continue to run large
and possibly unsustainable ﬁscal deﬁcits; it can ﬁnd some way to persuade
the private sector to spend more than its income once again; or it can try
(more plausibly) a mixture of both strategies. But if it is still running a cur-
rent account deﬁcit, politics are likely to lead it in a protectionist direction.



      • 210 •
                             From Imbalances to the Subprime Financial Crisis •



This would, alas, be the simplest way for the country to switch spending
toward domestic production, so reducing the proportion of ﬁnal demand
that leaks out through the trade deﬁcit. Why should it choose protection,
rather than a depreciation of the dollar against other currencies? The an-
swer is evident: other countries, China foremost among them, are not al-
lowing the needed decline in the dollar. On the contrary, they insist that
the United States continues to sustain the dollar’s value. The United States,
as issuer of the world’s key currency, cannot have an exchange rate policy
of its own. So it may well be driven toward protection instead.
     This analysis of the postcrisis situation also has profound implications
for China. Understandably, Chinese policy makers have resolutely rejected
any responsibility for the ﬁnancial crisis. In their view, the borrowers—and
the borrowers alone—bear responsibility for the failure to use wisely the
sums they borrowed. That is a defensible position, in theory, but it is not
a practical one. In practice, lenders have to be concerned about the cred-
itworthiness of their customers. If they are not, they are certain to end up
with losses. That is exactly what has now happened to China. It has lent
vast sums of money, in a foreign currency, to people who have not used
the money wisely. Indeed, they have used it so unwisely that a global ﬁ-
nancial crisis has resulted. This policy has turned out to be a mistake, as
the book argues at some length (see Chapter 6). True, the accumulation
of reserves has given the Chinese government the insurance it needs to
embark on a large stimulus program. In this respect, the program of self-
insurance has worked. But it is hard to believe that reserves worth over
$2 trillion were necessary for this purpose.
     The crisis has clariﬁed the implications of China’s policy choices, both
retrospectively and prospectively. Retrospectively, it is clear that a part of
China’s past growth was an illusion, in that it represented an exchange of
real goods for paper claims that are likely to be worth substantially less,
when redeemed, than they initially cost. More fundamentally, as the book
argues, a policy of relying on export-led growth, as recommended by ad-
vocates of Bretton Woods Two, is as unsustainable for a country as vast as
China as is a policy of open-ended borrowing for the United States. A pol-
icy that is appropriate for South Korea and feasible for Japan cannot be fol-
lowed by a country as vast as China. Now that it has run out of credit-
worthy high-income consumers for its exports, this point is evident.
     This means that China’s policy has to change. China’s immediate re-
sponse to the shock of the crisis has been to encourage a huge expansion
of domestic credit and investment. What it needs, in the longer term, is a
route to more balanced growth, with a greater role for hitherto suppressed
domestic consumption. As a consequence, the huge current account sur-
plus should diminish, facilitating global adjustment, while raising the un-



                                                                      • 211 •
    • Fixing Global Finance



necessarily low living standards of much of the Chinese population. This
will necessitate a program of domestic reform, which will raise public con-
sumption, household disposable incomes, and private consumption. Im-
portant elements in these reforms will be higher interest rates on bank
lending and deposits and higher taxation of dividends. The longer-term
aim should be to lower investment as a share of GDP, with the balance go-
ing into public and private consumption, particularly by the poorer mem-
bers of society. The alternative—further increases in the country’s already
massive level of investment—is likely to prove unsustainable in the long
run. It would certainly be wasteful.



    Policy Choices for the Longer Term
     In this book, I have identiﬁed as the most pressing need a system of
insurance that would allow emerging countries to run current account
deﬁcits without either fearing the unmanageable consequences of cur-
rency crises or deciding to accumulate vast quantities of foreign currency
reserves as self-insurance. This argument has been strongly supported by
Dominique Strauss-Kahn, managing director of the IMF.31 The obvious
need is to increase the resources of the IMF and provide low-conditional-
ity access to those resources to countries in need, in the event of a global
shock.
     A start in that direction was made in April 2009, following agreement
on a tripling of the IMF’s available resources, via the “New Arrangements
to Borrow” put into effect at the summit of the G-20 in London.32 Never-
theless, it was only a modest beginning: the world’s foreign currency re-
serves will still exceed regular IMF resources by almost an order of magni-
tude. Of equal importance, well-managed countries must be conﬁdent of
receiving the money they need at once, if they are to trust the insurance
provided by the IMF. The IMF’s new short-term liquidity facility, set up in
2008, is an important step.33 But it will probably need further development.
     A complementary proposal would be to make regular allocations of
special drawing rights (SDRs)—the IMF’s artiﬁcial reserve currency—to
emerging countries.34 This initiative was another product of the London
summit, with a decision for an immediate allocation of $250 billion. Reg-
ular issuance of SDRs to emerging and developing countries would allow
them to run current account deﬁcits safely, thus assisting the necessary and
desirable rebalancing of the world economy. Olivier Blanchard, chief econ-
omist of the IMF, is skeptical about the role played by imbalances in cre-
ating the crisis. But even he agrees that sustaining recovery “will require
delicate rebalancing acts, both within and across countries.”35 In retro-



    • 212 •
                             From Imbalances to the Subprime Financial Crisis •



spect, moreover, it is absurd that much of the world’s spare savings went
to ﬁnance unneeded construction of housing in some of the world’s rich-
est countries. It would have been far better if the money had gone to ﬁ-
nance necessary investment in the world’s emerging countries. Reforming
the international monetary system to achieve this goal is a high priority.
     Giving emerging and developing countries greater conﬁdence in the
IMF will also require changes in its governance. The rise of the G-20 to
dominance, itself a consequence of the crisis, increases pressure for a re-
weighting of votes and for changes in representation of countries on the
board of the IMF. Advanced countries will resist these changes. But they
have to be made if the IMF is to play a more effective role in the future.
Equally important will be a determination by the IMF to perform its sur-
veillance functions more aggressively. The painful truth is that the IMF was
dramatically unsuccessful in inﬂuencing policy before the crisis. It has an
opportunity to do better now. But if it tries to do so, it will certainly
run into ﬁerce opposition from certain powerful countries. Courage will
be necessary. Yet it may not be enough: the IMF’s inﬂuence over creditor
countries or over the United States is inevitably limited. But without
courage, the fund is sure to fail.
     Some argue for even more radical proposals: a move to replace the dol-
lar with a new global currency, along the lines of the single European cur-
rency, or a return to the gold standard. Neither of these ideas is practical
in the medium term. This will remain a world of national currencies for
the foreseeable future. But it is possible to envisage reforms that would
make such a world somewhat less unstable than it has been.



    Conclusion
     We have reached a turning point in the history of the world economy.
The arguments I develop in this book have become even more urgent and
important than at the time I ﬁrst wrote it. As I have argued in this chap-
ter, the crisis itself was caused, at least in part, by the way in which the
global ﬁnancial and monetary systems have worked, or failed to work. This
is not to deny the failures of ﬁnancial regulation and monetary policy in
the advanced countries. Nor is it to deny the need for radical reforms in
these areas. But something even bigger is at work.
     If the aftermath of the crisis is not to be unsustainable ﬁscal deﬁcits in
the erstwhile high-spending countries (as their governments become
spenders and borrowers of last resort), a resort to serious protectionism, or
both, signiﬁcant changes must now occur in the balance between the
growth of demand and supply across the globe. These changes are not a



                                                                      • 213 •
    • Fixing Global Finance



luxury; they are a necessity. If radical reforms are not made in the global
monetary and ﬁnancial systems over the next few years, there is a good
chance that the open world economy itself will founder. With it could well
go hopes for cooperative relations among the countries of the world and
for rapid rises in incomes for the world’s poor. The world economy has sur-
vived the crisis—but only just. The recovery is far from healthy and bal-
anced. The challenge ahead is huge, and it must be met. The alternative is
one we do not dare contemplate.

18. The balance of payments is deﬁned as follows. The balance of trade in goods
    is exports of goods less imports of goods. The balance of trade in nonfactor
    services (that is, services other than those of labor and capital) is exports of
    nonfactor services (such as management consulting, legal services, educa-
    tional services, or tourism) less imports of nonfactor services. The balance of
    trade in goods and services is the sum of the balance of trade in goods and
    the balance of trade in nonfactor services. The balance in income receipts is
    also known as the balance in factor services. Positive factor service income
    is income earned by residents abroad from their work or from the assets they
    own (as dividends, interest, or rent on portfolio or direct investment). Neg-
    ative sources of factor service income are the income earned by foreign resi-
    dents from factors of production employed in the domestic economy. Cur-
    rent transfers are gifts from foreign residents less gifts to foreign residents.
    Remittances are included as transfers (gifts), not as income from labor. Gifts
    from governments (e.g., aid) are also transfers. The current account then is the
    sum of the balance in goods and services, the balance in factor service in-
    come, and the balance in transfers. The current account is ﬁnanced by net
    capital ﬂows—foreign residents’ purchases of domestic assets (including pur-
    chases by foreign governments) less residents’ purchases of foreign assets (in-
    cluding purchases by the government). By deﬁnition, a current account
    deﬁcit is equal to a capital account surplus. In much of the analysis here and
    in subsequent chapters, the capital account drives the current account.
19. The relation between national income and the balance of payments needs
    to be analyzed brieﬂy, since it is central to any discussion of the macro-
    economic consequences of balance of payments adjustment.
       Gross domestic product is a measure of the gross value added, expenditure
    on gross value added, or income earned by factors of production within a



                                                                         • 219 •
    • Notes to Page 37



    given jurisdiction. Output, expenditure on output, and income earned from
    production are always equal, by deﬁnition. In simple form, GDP is also equal
    to private consumption plus government consumption plus private gross in-
    vestment plus government gross investment plus the balance of trade in
    goods and nonfactor services. Sales to nonresidents (i.e., exports) are a con-
    tribution to domestic output, while purchases from nonresidents (i.e., im-
    ports) subtract from domestic output. If one adds net factor income to GDP,
    one obtains gross national product (GNP). Thus GDP is a measure of the out-
    put, income, or expenditure on production within a given jurisdiction, while
    GNP is a measure of the income accruing to residents from economic activ-
    ity across the entire world. GDP belongs to a location, while GNP belongs to
    the people who live in a given location. If the current account, rather than
    the balance of trade in goods and nonfactor services, is added to consump-
    tion and investment, one obtains GNP rather than GDP.
       Now consider the absorption of resources by an economy, assuming for
    simplicity’s sake that net transfers are zero and that GDP is equal to GNP (i.e.,
    the balance of factor income is zero). Absorption measures the expenditures
    by residents on all goods and services. An economy’s absorption is domestic
    output (GDP) plus imports less exports. Imports less exports is a measure of
    the balance of trade (with the sign reversed). Thus absorption exceeds GDP
    by the size of the trade deﬁcit. The trade deﬁcit (equal in this case, by as-
    sumption, to the current account deﬁcit) must be ﬁnanced by a net inﬂow
    of capital or, in other words, a sale of claims on domestic assets (real prop-
    erty, bonds, and equity). It can continue only so long as it is possible to make
    such sales. It is clear then that if the net capital inﬂow falls, so must absorp-
    tion of resources in the economy—people must spend less.
       Finally, consider the uses of claims on income. In national accounts, these
    can be spent on private or government consumption, or saved, and so can
    be made available to investors at home or abroad. Since the uses of income
    must equal GDP, the difference between savings and domestic investment is
    the balance of trade. If residents save more than they invest at home, they
    accumulate assets abroad (i.e., run a current account surplus) and vice versa.
    The difference between savings and domestic investment is also equal to the
    difference between GDP and absorption, and that in turn is the current ac-
    count balance.

26. I elaborated this view in the discussion of the economists’ forum in my col-
    umn “Villains and Victims of Global Capital Flows.” The context was a discus-
    sion of the relevance of the IS-LM (investment–saving–liquidity preference–
    money supply) model of the late Sir John Hicks, in which macroeconomic
    activity is determined jointly by the level of investment, the propensity to
    save, the supply of money, and liquidity preference. My remarks were as fol-
    lows: “First, there has been an increase in the world’s (ex ante) propensity to
    save out of income and a decline in the (ex ante) propensity to invest, partly
    because of the bursting of the stock market bubble after 2000, partly because
    of the worldwide rise in proﬁt shares in GDP and partly because of the large
    shift in global incomes towards high-saving countries (i.e. East Asia and the
    oil exporters). All this generated a shift in the global IS curve down and to
    the left. If there had been no monetary policy response there would have
    been a global recession. But there was such a response in the signiﬁcant coun-
    tries. So the LM curve was also shifted downwards to the right. The new equi-
    librium point kept the world economy close to ‘full employment’ (itself, of
    course, a moving point in terms of output, given the expansion in global ca-
    pacity), but at lower nominal (and real) interest rates than before.”

    Ir – Sr , where S is saving, I is investment, u is the United States, and r is the
    rest of the world. Those who think the deﬁcits are the result of U.S. proﬂi-
    gacy stress the decline in Su . Those who think the “imbalances” are the re-
    sult of improved attractiveness of investment in the United States would em-
    phasize a rise in Iu . But since there has been no such rise, it cannot explain
    the current account deﬁcits. Those who emphasize the savings glut would



                                                                         • 223 •
   • Notes to Pages 111–112



   point to the rise in Sr . Such a rise has occurred in some countries, notably
   China and the oil exporters. Finally, those who emphasize the global in-
   vestment dearth point to declines in Ir . Those have also happened. The ar-
   gument here is that shifts in the relationship between Ir and Sr that have
   raised the surplus, partly driven by government decisions in the rest of the
   world, have led to policy shifts in the United States that, given the exoge-
   nous Iu , have generated a fall in Su . The latter fall is partly the result of an ex-
   ogenous decision by the U.S. government to raise cyclically adjusted ﬁscal
   deﬁcits and partly the result of decisions by the Federal Reserve. The hous-
   ing bubble was part of the transmission channel from U.S. monetary policy

   5 • Calm before a Storm

1. The remark is often mistakenly attributed to Charles de Gaulle, ﬁrst presi-

3. The fact that the nominal cost of liabilities was lower than the growth rate

   The principal explanation was the difference between the cost of U.S. liabil-
   ities and the return on U.S. assets abroad, which lowered the ratio of net li-
   abilities to GDP by 30 percentage points over this period. Speciﬁcally the U.S.
   beneﬁted from the relatively poor performance of the U.S. stock market, from
   the fall of the U.S. dollar (in which its liabilities are denominated) after 2001,
   and from the composition of its portfolio: higher-returning equity instru-
   ments (including FDI) made up roughly 60 percent of its assets and 40 per-
   cent of its liabilities. See International Monetary Fund, World Economic Out-
   look, April 2007.

   italist.” “Hedge fund” seems a broader concept. A hedge fund borrows to in-
   vest in relatively risky assets. That is what the United States as a whole does.

48. Consider only the U.S. example. In 2006 overall domestic purchases were
    some 106 percent of GDP; imports were about 17 percent of GDP; and ex-
    ports were about 11 percent of GDP. Assume that demand for imports falls
    proportionately with spending: a 1 percent fall in aggregate spending gen-
    erates a 1 percent decline in spending on imports. Assume no shifts in rela-
    tive prices and so no increase in the incentive to supply exports or import
    substitutes. Assume, more restrictively, no ability to shift import substitutes
    into exports. Assume too that the current account deﬁcit is to fall by 4 per-
    cent of GDP. Then imports need to fall from 17 percent to 13 percent of ini-
    tial GDP. How much does overall spending need to fall to achieve that out-
    come? The answer, based on these extremely simpliﬁed assumptions, is that
    domestic purchases need to fall by 26 percent and GDP by 21 percent to re-
    duce the current account deﬁcit by a mere 4 percent of GDP.




                                                                       • 227 •
    • Notes to Pages 151–164



       Assume instead that exports, imports, and import substitutes are per-
    fectly substitutable (and so can be aggregated into the standard broad
    category of “tradables”) and also that spending on tradables is 30 percent
    of purchases and so 32 percent of GDP and that the supply of tradables is
    26 percent of GDP (giving us, again, an external deficit of 6 percent of
    GDP). To reduce demand for tradables to 28 percent of GDP, domestic pur-
    chases must fall by 12.5 percent and GDP by 9.25 percent. Again, the result
    is a deep recession, though not as deep as in the previous example. This
    description is not purely theoretical. It is exactly what happened in the
    Asian financial crisis. It is bound to happen if a deficit must be eliminated
    quickly.

    term interest rates are already high by the standards of Japan and the Euro-
    zone. See Meissner and Taylor, “Losing Our Marbles.”
 4. Figure 6.2 shows that prices of tradables have been falling relative to those of
    nontradables. That is why the rise in the ratio of imports and exports to GDP
    has been much faster in constant prices than in actual prices. In real terms,
    since 1980 imports have tripled relative to GDP while exports have only dou-
    bled. This is what has to change if the deﬁcit is to stop rising relative to GDP.
    Given the starting point (with imports more than 50 percent higher than ex-
    ports), the deﬁcit would rise relative to GDP so long as trade grew faster than
    GDP, even if imports and exports grew at the same rate in real terms. So re-
    ducing the deﬁcit requires exports to grow substantially faster than imports.

10. John Cassidy provides a good explanation of the theories of this unorthodox
    economist in “The Minsky Moment.” In Minsky’s theory, the seven stages of
    a ﬁnancial crisis are displacement; higher prices in the affected sectors; cheap
    credit, often associated with ﬁnancial innovation; overtrading or specula-
    tion; euphoria; insider proﬁt-taking; and ﬁnally revulsion. It sounds famil-
    iar, does it not?
